The Succefs, indeed, of our Expedition 
was not fuch as might at firft have been 
expected from the Skill of our Commander, 
and the Refolution of our Men, Difa- 
greements and Mifmanagements having bro- 
ken our Meafures, and defeated our moft 
promifing Hopes s as they have often been 
Occafions of the Mifcarriage of the greatef 
and nobleft Attempts. 


BUT though we failed in our chief De- 
Jfigus, yet the; great Variety of Accidents 
ave met with, and the many Difcoveries 
we made, and particular Accounts of the 
Manner how our Atempts mifcarried, I 
hope caunot but be very acceptable to the in- 
quifitive Reader.

California, ¢o the Port of Valdivia, which 
zs above fixty Degrees, I have given one 
continued and much more partiwular De-

The Booby defcribed. The Ifland Lc Grand. 7 


the Main, about 4 Miles. This place is very much 42. 1703. 
troubled with Southerly Winds which blow extraor- 
® dinarily in Gufts ; therefore the only way is to lay 
® your beft Anchor to the Southward, and all little 
© enough fometimes. The Ifands produce nothing 
7 but Wood; and have a vaft many Sea-fowl upon 
+ them, by Sailors called Boobies, See a Draught of the _ See 
* three Iflands of St. Anns. Fig, 5. 
The Booby is much about the bignefs of a Duck; See 
_ fome are quite white, fome grey ; they have Feet Fig. 6. 
like a Duck, being a Water-fowl ; they feed mott- 
ly upon Flying-fith, which they catch flying, I 
have made many a Meal of this fort of Birds, but it 
was for want of other Victuals; They tafte very 
fithy ; and if you do not falt them very well before 
you eat them, they will make you fick 5 They are 
fo filly, that when they are weary of flying, they 
will, if you hold out your Hand, come and fit 
upon it: From thence I conjecture that they are 
called Boobies, 
November 24th we anchored at the Ifland Le 
Grand, whofe Latitude is 23 d. 30m. S, Longitude 
from London W. 40d. 24m. and found here about 
11d. E. Variation. This is a very woody Ifand, See 
and hath feveral very good Springs of frefh Water Fig, 6. 
) uponit. The Soil is black, and the IfMand is about 
9 Leagues round, and diftant from the Main about 
3 Miles. It is not inhabited by any other than Jac- 
= cils, Lyons, Tygers, €@c, which inthe Night make 
* amott hideous Noife, enough to terrify any Man. 
» About 3 Miles from this place is the main Land, all 
_ very mountainous and woody, where is a fmall 
- Town of the Portugueze (called by the Name ot 
I Grand Town,) who come out of the Country 
© for 40 or 50 Miles round on Saint Andrew’s Day, to 
» pay their Devotions to that Saint, here being a {mall 
Church confecrated to his ufe. ‘They abide here a- 
bout 14 Days, and then return to their own Dweil-

within the Nut is about a Pint anda half of pure 
clear Water, which is very cool, brisk, pleafant 
and fweet. The Kernel of the Nut is allo very 
good ; which if it be pretty old, we fcrape all to 

ieces ; the Scrapings we fet to foak in about a quart

have a thick Scum on it, not unlikeCream. This

Port of Guatulco is famous for its being taken by our 
Engh Hero Sir Francis Drake in the Year 1579; 
who in one Houte took a Bufhel of Money. Ic was 
afterwards taken and burnt by Sir 740, Cavendi/h in 
the Year 158%. But it has always been famous for

fuch Goods as they defign for Peru. *Tis alfo the

fund, called Tangola ; which is full of Hills and

From Cape Blanco to the Herradura, 13 9 Leagues ; 
between which is the Gulf of Nicoya. It is a place 
much ufed by the Spaniards, Capt. Sharp in this 
place, in the River De /a de/penza, took fome Spa- 
nfo Carpenters, who were building a Ship, and 
brought them to the Ifland Chira, with all their 


B Tools, on board of his own Ship, where they re- 


fitted her 3; and in the River Tempi/que they took 2 
Barks laden with Tallow. In this fame Gulf, in the 
Year 1704, near the Ifland St. Lucas, we took a 
Bark of about 40 Tuns. She had on Board fome 
Vine, Brandy and Sugar, witii 5 or 6 Indian Car- 
penters and Caulkers, who came in a good time to 
help us to clean our Ship, and torefit her, if we 
had Plank to do it with. Ata heap of fmall Iflands 


ying in the middle of the Gulf, called the Middle

As. 1704 from whence the Town of Nicoya is diftant about 19 
wy~ Leagues, confifting of about 70 or 80 Houtes, by. 
ing a mixture of Spaniards and Indians. This jg 
faid to be a pretty well compacted Town, and Head 
of a Spanifh Government. It is governed by an Al. 
cade or Mayor. From it come Fowls, Salt, Ho. 
ney and other Provifions, which they yearly fend 
to Panama in {mall Veffels. The Nicoyans are q 
People of a middle Stature, and tawny Complexi. 
vn. Before the coming of the Spaniards, they had 
very good Laws among themfelves ; only they had 
no Law againtt a Man’s killing bis Father, Mother, 
or King: They, as I fuppofe, thinking no Man 
would or could be fo very wicked, as to murder éi- 
ther his own Parents, or the common Father of 
them all, whom they ufed to adore as a God. A 
Thief they judged not todie, but to be madea 
Slave to the Man he robs, till by his Service he has 
made Satisfaction. This Gulf is very much infett- 
ed with Worms. To fome of thefe Iflands the Spa- 
ziaids trequently come to fifh for Pearl, of which 
they often make very profitable Voyages. Here are 
alfo vait Numbers of extraordinary good Sea-tur-

T the Point de Chaine begins the great Bay of 
Panama ; which is the greateft Sea--port, next 

mi, of any on the Welt-fide of this great Conti- 
nt of America, It is faid to have eight Parith 
urches, thirty fmall Chapels, and to contain fix 
houfand and five Hundred Houfes, moft of them 
!| built of Brick and Stone. It is very well for- 
ied, and walled all round with Stone: The City 
governed by a Prefident under the Viceroy of 
ri, it being part of his Territories. On the 
k-fide of the City is a very pleafant and fruit- 

|Country. The Land is not very high, but full 
fmall Hills, and fine pleafant Valleys, in which 
ere are feveral fine Gardens of feveral forts of 
i. This is a place of the greateft Trade of any 

the South-Seas, not excepting even Lima the Me- 
polis. For this City has the conveniency of 
ade both from the North and South-Sea. From 
North-Sea, it receives all, or moft of the Exro- 

ys Goods which are brought by the Spaniards to 
rtobel or Carthagena, or by any other Nation 
ding on the North-fide : And thele Luropean 
bods are from hence by Shipping in the South-Sea 
itto Guiaguill, Truxillo, Lima, Avica, Coguimbo, 
anadore, Valpirizo and Valdivia, with feveral o- 

t noted Places; from whence they return back 
hly laden with the Commodities of thefe Parts. 
t now the French having a Trade amongft them, 
i fupplying them with all European Neceffaries, 
inders this City mightily ; and the § paniards, as 

as we could hear, whilft we were in thefe Parts, 

M rather have their room than their Company. 
wever, the French at prefent make very great 
i profitable Voyages; and now that they find 
fweet of it, they will be fure, if they can, to 

tle a firm and lafting Trade here. For, as we

| On the, 2d: of February we had a Calm moft

one middling One underneath near the Gills, one

gocs in with a Dent, and eleven fmall ones

bbone, ~“Thefe ith, when t ken by us, looked

The Birds of Paradice are about the Bignefs 
of a Pidgeon. ha are of various Colours, and 
are never found or feen alive; neither is it known 
from whence they come. I have feen feveral of 
| them here embalm’d with Spice, which preferves 
them from Decay ; and fo emblam’d they are 
{ent as Rarities to feveral Parts of the World. 
It is related of thefe Birds, that when the Nut- 
megs are ripe, which is in the Months of Fe- 
brusry and March, they refort to the Places 
where they grow, viz. to Banda and this Place, 
and eat of the outer Rind of the Nut; atter 
which they fall down dead drunk, and an innu- 
merable Company of Ants gather about them, 
and feed upon them, and kill them.

An. 17°5 the Dutch kill a Beeve ora Sheep, they will beg 
the Guts, out of which they fqueeze the Dung,

Vheir Children, when they are young, ar 
fomething inclining to be white 5 and were it

moft of them very «well featur’d. The Dutch did

-nafty Way of Living; but never could prevail | 
with them, they ftill defiring rather to live like 
Beafts. Worthip they feem to have none, cx 
cept at the full Moons, and then they dance 
and fing all or moft of the Night; and the 
brighter the Moon is, the more 1s their Mirth; | 
tor then they think the Moon, which feems to 
be their God, is well-pleafed with them: Butif 
the Moon chance to be obfcured with Clouds 
then they feem much dejected, and fancy their 
God is angry with them.

On the 20th we found ourfelves in the Lati-

Showcrs of Snow or Hail, and the Weather very

the Weather very fair and moderate; the Ree 
fon of which, I fuppofe, was this: When we 
were to the Southward, we were always pretty 
near to the Main of Americz, having it to the 
Weft of us: Likewife when we were to the 
Northward, we were always pretty near the 


main Land of Europe, having it to the Haft of 


us. Now being near the Land, we always a 
count the Land-winds the coldeft, and the vew 
Winds the warmeft. Thus the North-Hatterly 


Wind is accounted the coldeft Wind we havei
